Adding Negation to Lambda Mu
Steffen van Bakel ORCID:0000-0003-2077-011X

Abstract
. We present ℒ {\mathcal{L}} , an extension of Parigot’s λ ​ μ {\lambda\mu} -calculus by adding negation as a type constructor, together with syntactic constructs that represent negation introduction and elimination. We will define a notion of reduction that extends λ ​ μ {\lambda\mu} ’s reduction system with two new reduction rules, and show that the system satisfies subject reduction. Using Aczel’s generalisation of Tait and Martin-Löf’s notion of parallel reduction, we show that this extended reduction is confluent. Although the notion of type assignment has its limitations with respect to representation of proofs in natural deduction with implication and negation, ⊢ ni \mathrel{\vdash_{\kern-2.0pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\scriptsize\sc ni}}} , we will show that all propositions that can be shown in ⊢ ni \mathrel{\vdash_{\kern-2.0pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\scriptsize\sc ni}}} have a witness in ℒ {\mathcal{L}} . Using Girard’s approach of reducibility candidates, we show that all typeable terms are strongly normalisable, and conclude the paper by showing that type assignment for ℒ {\mathcal{L}} enjoys the principal typing property.



Sep. 23, 2021May 25, 2023

†
†
lmcs-doi:
Volume 19, Issue 2, Paper 12

Introduction

Intuitionistic Logic (
il
)
[
Bro07
,
Bro08
,
Bro75
]
plays an important role in Computer Science, given its strong relation with types in functional programming and the
\lambda
-calculus
[
Cur34
,
Bar84
]
through the Curry-Howard isomorphism
[
How80
]
,
i.e.
through the fact that typeable functions in a functional programming language correspond to proofs in
il
, and provable properties to inhabitable types.
Its importance is most prominent in the context of proof assistants, of which many are rooted in
il
.
Proof assistants or theorem provers can also be seen as programming languages for which the type system corresponds to a formal logic and ensure proof correctness by capitalising on the Curry-Howard correspondence through their type system.
Under this correspondence, checking that a term has a type is operationally equivalent to checking a proof of a proposition
[
Wad15
]
.

There are currently many different proof assistants in use, that come in different shapes and forms, each with their own characteristic: Coq
[
Coq21
]
has a particular focus on the theorem proving aspect where proofs can be written with intuitive tactics, whereas Agda
[
Nor07
]
and Idris
[
Bra13
]
are more deeply connected to functional programming languages like Haskell.

The more widely used proof assistants that are based on the Curry-Howard isomorphism are all founded on
intuitionistic type theory

[
Mar84
]
.
However, the use of
il
inescapably limits these languages to the fact that they are unable to prove a simple notion, which use is widespread in normal, everyday mathematics: each proposition is either true or false.
This is known as the law of the excluded middle (
lem
), and is the distinguishing feature of Classical Logic (
cl
)
[
Gen35
,
Sza69
]
.
There are theorem provers that use classical logic, like Trybulec’s Mizar
[
TB85
]
, but that is not founded in the Curry-Howard isomorphism; for an overview of proof assistants and their background, see
[
Geu09
]
.

It can be argued that
il
very rightly rejects this notion and there are many that do exactly that: they stress the value of
il
, where a proof of ‘
A
or
B
’ must be
constructive
,
i.e.
constructed from a proof of either
A
or from a proof of
B
, so stating ‘
A
or not
A
’, without justifying either first, is unacceptable.
Likewise, a proof for the statement
\exists\,x\mathbin{\in}C\,(\hskip 0.5ptQ(x)\hskip 0.5pt)
is only acceptable if first
Q(c)
is shown, for some object
c\mathbin{\in}C
(
i.e.
a
witness
for
Q
has been produced).
Therefore
\neg\forall\,x\mathbin{\in}C\,(\hskip 0.5ptP(x)\hskip 0.5pt)\mathrel{\hskip 1.0pt\Rightarrow\hskip 1.0pt}\exists\,x\mathbin{\in}C\,(\hskip 0.5pt\neg P(x)\hskip 0.5pt)
cannot be shown in
il
, since knowing that there has to be an element in
C
for which
P
does not hold is not the same as knowing which element that is.
Accepting
il
as the basis for mathematical reasoning, which for many is the only right thing to do for philosophical reasons, severely limits the collection of provable results, and is therefore not a popular choice amongst mathematicians.
Some theorem provers, perhaps begrudgingly, allow for the addition of the axiom ‘
A
or not
A
’, witnessed through a term constant; although it allows for provability of mathematical statements, this approach does not lend computational context to proofs, as theorem provers for
il
do, and does not really adhere to the Curry-Howard isomorphism.

In fact, the popularity of
il
, constructive logic and constructive mathematics in computer science can be explained through its strong ties with computability through the Curry-Howard correspondence and the relation between
il
, the
\lambda
-calculus and functional programming.
That situation changed when Griffin
[
Gri90
]
observed that the
C
-operator of Felleisen’s
\lambda C
-calculus
[
FH92
]
, similar to the
call/cc
function in
Scheme
, can be typed with
\neg\neg A\mathop{\rightarrow}A
(or rather
((A\mathop{\rightarrow}{\perp})\mathop{\rightarrow}{\perp})\mathop{\rightarrow}A
),
double negation elimination
, another property that only holds in
cl
, thus highlighting the first link between
cl
and sequential control in computer science.
This soon led to the definition of
{\lambda\mu}
by Parigot
[
Par92
,
Par93a
]
, a calculus that represents minimal classical logic
[
AHS07
]
, followed by an impressive body of work in the area of
cl
and computer science, with many contributions from various authors.

Looking to investigate the possibility and suitability of developing theorem provers for
cl
based on
{\lambda\mu}
, in
[
DvBW21
]
the case was made that in terms of implementability, expressiveness, and elegance, proof assistants based on
cl
have much to contribute.
It presented
Candid
, a theorem prover based on
{\lambda\mu}
, but enriched with dependent types, as an extension of
\textrm{ECC}_{\textrm{\scriptsize K}}

[
MMMM20
]
adding co-products and dependent algebraic data types.
It treated a system of classical natural deduction that uses the logical connectors
implication
,
negation
,
conjunction
, and
disjunction
.

As seen in that paper, the link between first order classical logic and computation is tricky.
Theorem provers are based in dependent-types systems, but
[
Her05
]
showed that by naively combining dependent types and
{\lambda\mu}
’s control operators, all types have only one inhabitant.
Fortunately,
[
Her12
]
shows a way to restrict how dependent types and control operators interact, which regains a logically consistent type theory.
An important notion to address this problem is the use of
negative elimination free
(
nef
) terms that cannot contain a negation elimination.
Since in
{\lambda\mu}
negation elimination gets represented through application, as well as through
naming
(see Example
2.11
), this restriction is quite drastic.
Although it is unavoidable for a
nef
term to not contain sub-terms of the form
\mu\hskip 0.25pt\alpha\hskip 0.25pt{.}\hskip 0.25pt[\beta]\hskip 0.5ptN
as the subterm
[\beta]N
corresponds with an application of
(\neg E)
, it also cannot contain an application
{M}\hskip 0.5ptN
, as this could correspond with
(\neg E)
when
M
has type
A\mathord{\rightarrow}{\perp}
and
N
has type
A
.
Introducing separate syntax for negation, as done here, strongly expands the set of
nef
-terms to those really not dealing with negation, and will strengthen the implementation of
Candid
.

Another reason to deal with negation explicitly is the fact that
{\lambda\mu}
does not really represent
cl
, in that tautologies are not necessarily represented by closed terms.
This is in part due to the fact the system only has implicit negation and ‘proof by contradiction’
({\textsl{PbC}})
to express dealing with conflict, so negation
\neg A
is expressed through
A\mathord{\rightarrow}{\perp}
(where
{\perp}
is not a type in the original presentation), negation introduction through abstraction and negation elimination through application.
For example, in Example
2.11
we will show Parigot’s proof for double negation elimination in
{\lambda\mu}
; the witness
\lambda y\hskip 0.25pt{.}\hskip 0.25pt\mu\hskip 0.25pt\alpha\hskip 0.25pt{.}\hskip 0.25pt[\gamma]\hskip 0.5pt{y}\hskip 0.5pt({\lambda x\hskip 0.25pt{.}\hskip 0.25pt\mu\hskip 0.25pt\delta\hskip 0.25pt{.}\hskip 0.25pt[\alpha]\hskip 0.5ptx})
contains a free name
\gamma
of type
{\perp}
.
It is needed because the subterm
{y}\hskip 0.5pt({\lambda x\hskip 0.25pt{.}\hskip 0.25pt\mu\hskip 0.25pt\delta\hskip 0.25pt{.}\hskip 0.25pt[\alpha]\hskip 0.5ptx})
has type
{\perp}
, and the only way to deal with that in
{\lambda\mu}
is applying the rule for
({\textsl{PbC}})
, which forces the prefix
\mu\alpha.[\gamma]
to the term.
We will see that, dealing explicitly with negation, this problem disappears.

One of the strengths of
{\lambda\mu}
is that Call-by-Name reduction (
cbn
) is confluent, as shown by Py
[
Py98
]
.
Summers
[
Sum08
]
defines
\nu\lambda\mu
, a variant of
{\lambda\mu}
that deals with negation as well, but represents also Call-by-Value reduction (
cbv
), rendering reduction non-confluent.
The choice we make here is to aim for confluence, so reduction in the calculus
{\mathcal{L}}
we present here does not model
cbv
reduction, as is the case for Parigot’s
{\lambda\mu}
.
This paper presents
{\mathcal{L}}
and shows all the necessary properties for it, like soundness, confluence, expressiveness, termination, and principal typing.

Overview

This paper introduces the calculus
{\mathcal{L}}
, which expands on
{\lambda\mu}
by adding negation.
We will start in Section
1
with an overview of two of the common representations of
cl
, where we will focus on natural deduction and proof contraction, and why double negation elimination poses a particular problem for the latter.
We will define
\mathrel{\vdash_{\kern-2.0pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\scriptsize\sc ni}}}
, a restriction to natural deduction for
cl
that uses negation and implication, and plays a central role in this paper.
We will revisit Parigot’s
{\lambda\mu}
also through its underlying logic, and explain how it deals with negation, implicitly through assumptions stored in the co-context, and explicitly through
\mathop{\cdot}\mathord{\rightarrow}{\perp}
.
We in particular highlight that
{\lambda\mu}
is not fully equipped to deal with the latter kind of negation, as witnesses to tautologies not necessarily are closed terms.
We also revisit Summer’s
\nu\lambda\mu
-calculus that fully represents
\mathrel{\vdash_{\kern-2.0pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\scriptsize\sc ni}}}
, together with its non-confluent notion of reduction.

In Section
3
we define the calculus
{\mathcal{L}}
as an extension of
{\lambda\mu}
by adding syntax and inference rules that express negation; it can also be seen as a restriction of
\nu\lambda\mu
.
This calculus comes with four elementary notions of reduction, and we will show soundness results for all of them.
This is followed in Section
4
by the proof that reduction is confluent, and in Section
5
by the proof that, although a restriction of
\nu\lambda\mu
,
{\mathcal{L}}
can still inhabit all provable judgements of
\mathrel{\vdash_{\kern-2.0pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\scriptsize\sc ni}}}
.
Then in Section
6
, we will show that reduction is strongly normalisable, and conclude in Section
7
by showing that type assignment enjoys the principal typing property.

1.
Natural Deduction for Classical Logic

Natural Deduction for
cl
, defined by Gentzen in
[
Gen35
]
is a way of describing the structure of formal proofs in mathematics that follow the intuitive, human, lines of reasoning as much as possible.
It is defined through
inference rules
that are generically of the shape

Premisses

(\textit{Rule})

Conclusion

and describes a step allowed in this formal system, where, assuming that all the statements in the premisses hold, then after applying this step named
(\textit{Rule})
we can accept that the conclusion holds as well.
A statement, also called a judgement, is of the shape
{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{A}
, where
A
is a formula and
\Gamma
is a
context
, a collection of formulas that form the assumptions needed for
A
to hold, and expresses that ‘if all formulas in the collection
\Gamma
hold, then so does
A
’.
A number of these can together form the premisses; there is only one judgement in the conclusion.
1
1

1

An different notation can be found in the literature, where inference rules express the relation between the inferred formulas, without stating the context, and the assumptions are the formulas occurring in the leaves of the derivation tree.
Assumptions can be cancelled through steps like ‘implication introduction’, and are then placed between square brackets or struck through.
Since the latter is a non-local operation on the inference tree that is not easily defined or treated formally, here we prefer the ‘sequent’ notation: it neatly collects in the derived statement the assumptions on which it depends in the context
\Gamma
.

Proofs are constructed by applying rules to each other, in the sense that the conclusion of one rule can be a premise of another.
The premises on the initial rules (that are not the conclusion of other rules) are called the assumptions of the proof; the (single) conclusion occurs at the bottom.
Judgements that are considered to be proven are those that appear at the bottom of the derivation tree.

The inference rules of natural deduction systems almost all come in two varieties for each logical operator: introduction and elimination rules, each for any particular logical connective.
For example, for the logical operators
\mathbin{\wedge}
(
conjunction
) and
\mathbin{\vee}
(
disjunction
), these rules look like:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\tquad}l}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\penalty{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{A}\quad{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{B}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=49.8216pt}
\hbox{\kern 0.975pt${\mbox{$({\wedge}\textsl{I})$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 7.60349pt\hbox{${\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{A\mathbin{\wedge}B}$}}}}\quad\quad\quad&{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\penalty{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{A\mathbin{\wedge}B}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=34.12714pt}
\hbox{}}}\hbox{\kern 7.133pt\hbox{${\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{A}$}}}}\quad{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\penalty{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{A\mathbin{\wedge}B}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=34.13933pt}
\hbox{\kern 0.95062pt${\mbox{$({\wedge}\textsl{E})$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 6.84569pt\hbox{${\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{B}$}}}}\\[11.38109pt]
{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 7.6205pt\hbox{$\penalty{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{A}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=34.61464pt}
\hbox{}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{${\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{A\mathbin{\vee}B}$}}}}\quad{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 7.321pt\hbox{$\penalty{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{B}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=34.61462pt}
\hbox{\kern 0.95062pt${\mbox{$({\vee}\textsl{I})$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{${\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{A\mathbin{\vee}B}$}}}}\quad\quad\quad&{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\penalty{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{A\mathbin{\vee}B}\quad{\Gamma\kern-0.975pt,A}\mathrel{\vdash}{C}\quad{\Gamma\kern-0.975pt,B}\mathrel{\vdash}{C}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=119.05005pt}
\hbox{\kern 0.975pt${\mbox{$({\vee}\textsl{E})$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 49.41322pt\hbox{${\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{C}$}}}}\end{array}

To deal with conclusions that need no premisses since they hold by themselves, an
axiom
rule is added; these form the assumptions of the proof and occur in the ‘leaves’ of the proof tree.

({\textsl{Ax}})

{\Gamma\kern-0.975pt,A}\mathrel{\vdash}{A}

In his paper, Gentzen also presents the Sequent Calculus, which differs from Natural Deduction in that it derives sequences of the shape

A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}\vdash B_{1},\ldots,B_{m}

with the intended meaning ‘if all of the properties
A_{1}
, …,
A_{n}
hold, then at least one of the
B_{1}
, …,
B_{m}
does as well.’
For each connector, there is a
left
and
right
introduction rule, as in

\begin{array}[]{c@{\quad}c@{\tquad}c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 7.6205pt\hbox{$\penalty\Gamma\kern-0.975pt,A\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=47.39241pt}
\hbox{}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma\kern-0.975pt,A\mathbin{\wedge}B\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta$}}}}&{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 7.3271pt\hbox{$\penalty\Gamma\kern-0.975pt,B\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=47.39241pt}
\hbox{\kern 0.975pt${\mbox{$({\wedge}\textsl{L})$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma\kern-0.975pt,A\mathbin{\wedge}B\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta$}}}}\hfil\quad\quad\quad&{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\penalty\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}A,\Delta\quad\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}B,\Delta$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=75.37714pt}
\hbox{\kern 0.975pt${\mbox{$({\wedge}\textsl{R})$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 13.99237pt\hbox{$\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}A\mathbin{\wedge}B,\Delta$}}}}\end{array}

There are no elimination rules for connectors, just a generic
(\textsl{cut})
-rule:

\penalty\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}C,\Delta\quad\Gamma\kern-0.975pt,C\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta

(\textsl{cut})

\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\Delta

where
C
of course can be
A\mathbin{\wedge}B
.

For the Sequent Calculus, Gentzen defines a notion of (proof) contraction that removes occurrences of
(\textsl{cut})
, and shows that this is (weakly) normalising: for every proof that shows
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash_{\kern-2.0pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\scriptsize\sc lk}}}\Delta
, there exists a
(\textsl{cut})
-free proof that shows the same result.
The proof follows a left-most, innermost reduction strategy; it is not shown that cut-elimination is strongly normalising.
He does not show a normalisation result in
[
Gen35
]
for Natural Deduction, which would eliminate all introduction-elimination pairs, but there is evidence that he did solve that later
[
vP08
]
.
2
2

2

One particular difficulty with defining proof contractions on either the Sequent Calculus or Natural Deduction is that this notion is not
confluent
, in that proof contraction not always leads to the same result.

Prawitz
[
Pra65
]
presented an extensive study of proof contraction for Natural Deduction.

The main issue is that in the Sequent Calculus, all logical connectors come with a left and a right introduction rule, whereas in Natural Deduction, not all proof-constructions follow the introduction-elimination pattern of the inference rules.
For those that do, proof contraction consists of the removal from a proof of an introduction step followed immediately by an elimination step for the same logical connector; for ‘
\mathbin{\wedge}
’ that looks like:

\begin{array}[]{ccc@{\qquad}cccc}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\penalty\begin{picture}(-113.0,0.0)\put(0.0,100.0){\begin{picture}\put(-113.0,160.0){\line{1}{0}{226.0}}\put(-113.0,160.0){\line{1}{-4}{35.0}}\put(113.0,160.0){\line{-1}{-4}{35.0}}\put(-83.0,20.0){\line{1}{0}{166.0}}\end{picture}}{}\put(0.0,195.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\mbox{$D_{1}$}}}\put(0.0,-30.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,120.0){\hbox{${{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{A}}$}}}\end{picture}\quad\begin{picture}(-116.0,0.0)\put(0.0,100.0){\begin{picture}\put(-116.0,160.0){\line{1}{0}{232.0}}\put(-116.0,160.0){\line{1}{-4}{35.0}}\put(116.0,160.0){\line{-1}{-4}{35.0}}\put(-86.0,20.0){\line{1}{0}{172.0}}\end{picture}}{}\put(0.0,195.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\mbox{$D_{2}$}}}\put(0.0,-30.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,120.0){\hbox{${{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{B}}$}}}\end{picture}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=78.41374pt}
\hbox{\kern 0.95062pt${\mbox{$({\wedge}\textsl{I})$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 21.89955pt\hbox{${\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{A\mathbin{\wedge}B}$}}}}\>\penalty$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=106.81517pt}
\hbox{\kern 0.975pt${\mbox{$({\wedge}\textsl{E})$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 43.477pt\hbox{${\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{A}$}}}}&\mathrel{\hskip 1.0pt\Rightarrow\hskip 1.0pt}&\begin{picture}(-110.0,0.0)\put(0.0,100.0){\begin{picture}\put(-110.0,160.0){\line{1}{0}{220.0}}\put(-110.0,160.0){\line{1}{-4}{35.0}}\put(110.0,160.0){\line{-1}{-4}{35.0}}\put(-80.0,20.0){\line{1}{0}{160.0}}\end{picture}}{}\put(0.0,195.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\mbox{$D_{1}$}}}\put(0.0,-30.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,120.0){\hbox{${{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{A}}$}}}\end{picture}\hfil\quad\quad\quad\quad&{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\penalty\begin{picture}(-113.0,0.0)\put(0.0,100.0){\begin{picture}\put(-113.0,160.0){\line{1}{0}{226.0}}\put(-113.0,160.0){\line{1}{-4}{35.0}}\put(113.0,160.0){\line{-1}{-4}{35.0}}\put(-83.0,20.0){\line{1}{0}{166.0}}\end{picture}}{}\put(0.0,195.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\mbox{$D_{1}$}}}\put(0.0,-30.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,120.0){\hbox{${{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{A}}$}}}\end{picture}\quad\begin{picture}(-116.0,0.0)\put(0.0,100.0){\begin{picture}\put(-116.0,160.0){\line{1}{0}{232.0}}\put(-116.0,160.0){\line{1}{-4}{35.0}}\put(116.0,160.0){\line{-1}{-4}{35.0}}\put(-86.0,20.0){\line{1}{0}{172.0}}\end{picture}}{}\put(0.0,195.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\mbox{$D_{2}$}}}\put(0.0,-30.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,120.0){\hbox{${{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{B}}$}}}\end{picture}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=78.41374pt}
\hbox{\kern 0.95062pt${\mbox{$({\wedge}\textsl{I})$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 21.89955pt\hbox{${\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{A\mathbin{\wedge}B}$}}}}\>\penalty$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=106.81517pt}
\hbox{\kern 0.975pt${\mbox{$({\wedge}\textsl{E})$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 43.18361pt\hbox{${\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{B}$}}}}&\mathrel{\hskip 1.0pt\Rightarrow\hskip 1.0pt}&\begin{picture}(-113.0,0.0)\put(0.0,100.0){\begin{picture}\put(-113.0,160.0){\line{1}{0}{226.0}}\put(-113.0,160.0){\line{1}{-4}{35.0}}\put(113.0,160.0){\line{-1}{-4}{35.0}}\put(-83.0,20.0){\line{1}{0}{166.0}}\end{picture}}{}\put(0.0,195.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\mbox{$D_{2}$}}}\put(0.0,-30.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,120.0){\hbox{${{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{B}}$}}}\end{picture}\end{array}

or, for implication:

\begin{array}[]{c@{\qquad}cc@{\quad}c}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.52156pt\hbox{$\penalty{\Gamma\kern-0.975pt,A}\mathrel{\vdash}{B}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=32.94798pt}
\hbox{\kern 0.975pt${\mbox{$({\mathord{\rightarrow}\textsl{I}\hskip 0.975pt})$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{${\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{A\mathop{\rightarrow}B}$}}}}\\[19.91692pt]
{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\penalty{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{A\mathop{\rightarrow}B}\quad{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{A}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=62.32162pt}
\hbox{\kern 0.975pt${\mbox{$({\mathord{\rightarrow}\textsl{E}\hskip 0.38998pt})$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 20.93684pt\hbox{${\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{B}$}}}}\end{array}\hfil\quad\quad\quad\quad&{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 16.1287pt\hbox{$\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=31.80556pt}
\hbox{\kern 0.90367pt${\mbox{$({\textsl{Ax}})$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{${\Gamma\kern-0.90367pt,A}\mathrel{\vdash}{A}$}}}}\>\penalty$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=62.73108pt}
\hbox{}}}\hbox{\kern 14.14328pt\hbox{$\begin{picture}(-189.0,0.0)\put(0.0,100.0){\begin{picture}\put(-230.0,160.0){\line{1}{-4}{35.0}}\put(230.0,160.0){\line{-1}{-4}{35.0}}\put(-200.0,20.0){\line{1}{0}{400.0}}\end{picture}}{}\put(0.0,195.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\mbox{$D_{1}$}}}\put(0.0,-30.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,120.0){\hbox{${{\Gamma\kern-0.85555pt,A}\mathrel{\vdash}{B}}$}}}\end{picture}$}}}}\>\penalty$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=73.0891pt}
\hbox{\kern 0.95062pt${\mbox{$({\mathord{\rightarrow}\textsl{I}\hskip 0.95062pt})$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 20.07056pt\hbox{${\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{A\mathop{\rightarrow}B}$}}}}\>\penalty~\begin{picture}(-113.0,0.0)\put(0.0,100.0){\begin{picture}\put(-113.0,160.0){\line{1}{0}{226.0}}\put(-113.0,160.0){\line{1}{-4}{35.0}}\put(113.0,160.0){\line{-1}{-4}{35.0}}\put(-83.0,20.0){\line{1}{0}{166.0}}\end{picture}}{}\put(0.0,195.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\mbox{$D_{2}$}}}\put(0.0,-30.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,120.0){\hbox{${{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{A}}$}}}\end{picture}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=138.54892pt}
\hbox{\kern 0.975pt${\mbox{$({\mathord{\rightarrow}\textsl{E}\hskip 0.38998pt})$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 59.05049pt\hbox{${\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{B}$}}}}&\mathrel{\hskip 1.0pt\Rightarrow\hskip 1.0pt}&{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.24376pt\hbox{$\penalty\begin{picture}(-110.0,0.0)\put(0.0,100.0){\begin{picture}\put(-110.0,160.0){\line{1}{0}{220.0}}\put(-110.0,160.0){\line{1}{-4}{35.0}}\put(110.0,160.0){\line{-1}{-4}{35.0}}\put(-80.0,20.0){\line{1}{0}{160.0}}\end{picture}}{}\put(0.0,195.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\mbox{$D_{2}$}}}\put(0.0,-30.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,120.0){\hbox{${{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{A}}$}}}\end{picture}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=34.44453pt}
\hbox{}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\begin{picture}(-113.0,0.0)\put(0.0,100.0){\begin{picture}\put(-140.0,160.0){\line{1}{-4}{35.0}}\put(140.0,160.0){\line{-1}{-4}{35.0}}\put(-110.0,20.0){\line{1}{0}{220.0}}\end{picture}}{}\put(0.0,195.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\mbox{$D_{1}^{\prime}$}}}\put(0.0,-30.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,120.0){\hbox{${{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{B}}$}}}\end{picture}$}}}}\end{array}

Notice that, in the rule
({\mathord{\rightarrow}\textsl{I}\hskip 1.0pt})
, the formula
A
ceases to be an assumption, and that, in the composed proof on the right,
A
is no longer an assumption needed to reach the conclusion, since it has been shown to hold by
D_{2}
.
As a result
\Delta_{1}
and
\Delta_{1}^{\prime}
are not identical, since dealing with different contexts; however, the have the same structure in terms of rules applied.

This is not possible for all logical connectors: the way negation is dealt with is, for example, not straightforward.
Negation comes of course with introduction and elimination rules:

\begin{array}[]{c@{\qquad}cc@{\quad}c}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\penalty{\Gamma\kern-0.975pt,A}\mathrel{\vdash}{{\perp}}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=31.31805pt}
\hbox{\kern 0.975pt${\mbox{$({\neg\textsl{I}\hskip 0.38998pt})$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 1.5618pt\hbox{${\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{\neg A}$}}}}\\[19.91692pt]
{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\penalty{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{\neg A}\quad{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{A}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=57.56812pt}
\hbox{\kern 0.975pt${\mbox{$({\neg\textsl{E}\hskip 0.38998pt})$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 18.8535pt\hbox{${\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{{\perp}}$}}}}\end{array}\hfil\quad\quad\quad\quad&{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 16.1287pt\hbox{$\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=31.80556pt}
\hbox{\kern 0.90367pt${\mbox{$({\textsl{Ax}})$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{${\Gamma\kern-0.90367pt,A}\mathrel{\vdash}{A}$}}}}\>\penalty$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=62.73108pt}
\hbox{}}}\hbox{\kern 14.14328pt\hbox{$\begin{picture}(-185.0,0.0)\put(0.0,100.0){\begin{picture}\put(-230.0,160.0){\line{1}{-4}{35.0}}\put(230.0,160.0){\line{-1}{-4}{35.0}}\put(-200.0,20.0){\line{1}{0}{400.0}}\end{picture}}{}\put(0.0,195.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\mbox{$D_{1}$}}}\put(0.0,-30.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,120.0){\hbox{${{\Gamma\kern-0.85555pt,A}\mathrel{\vdash}{{\perp}}}$}}}\end{picture}$}}}}\>\penalty$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=73.0891pt}
\hbox{\kern 0.95062pt${\mbox{$({\neg\textsl{I}\hskip 0.38023pt})$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 22.44731pt\hbox{${\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{\neg A}$}}}}\>\penalty~\begin{picture}(-113.0,0.0)\put(0.0,100.0){\begin{picture}\put(-113.0,160.0){\line{1}{0}{226.0}}\put(-113.0,160.0){\line{1}{-4}{35.0}}\put(113.0,160.0){\line{-1}{-4}{35.0}}\put(-83.0,20.0){\line{1}{0}{166.0}}\end{picture}}{}\put(0.0,195.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\mbox{$D_{2}$}}}\put(0.0,-30.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,120.0){\hbox{${{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{A}}$}}}\end{picture}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=139.6452pt}
\hbox{\kern 0.975pt${\mbox{$({\neg\textsl{E}\hskip 0.38998pt})$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 59.89204pt\hbox{${\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{{\perp}}$}}}}&\mathrel{\hskip 1.0pt\Rightarrow\hskip 1.0pt}&{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.24376pt\hbox{$\penalty\begin{picture}(-110.0,0.0)\put(0.0,100.0){\begin{picture}\put(-110.0,160.0){\line{1}{0}{220.0}}\put(-110.0,160.0){\line{1}{-4}{35.0}}\put(110.0,160.0){\line{-1}{-4}{35.0}}\put(-80.0,20.0){\line{1}{0}{160.0}}\end{picture}}{}\put(0.0,195.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\mbox{$D_{2}$}}}\put(0.0,-30.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,120.0){\hbox{${{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{A}}$}}}\end{picture}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=34.44453pt}
\hbox{}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\begin{picture}(-110.0,0.0)\put(0.0,100.0){\begin{picture}\put(-140.0,160.0){\line{1}{-4}{35.0}}\put(140.0,160.0){\line{-1}{-4}{35.0}}\put(-110.0,20.0){\line{1}{0}{220.0}}\end{picture}}{}\put(0.0,195.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0){\mbox{$D_{1}$}}}\put(0.0,-30.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,120.0){\hbox{${{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{{\perp}}}$}}}\end{picture}$}}}}\end{array}

but, in Classical Logic, negation plays a more intricate role, in that the
law of excluded middle
‘
A\mathbin{\vee}\neg A
is true for all
A
’ holds (or something similar, like ‘there is no distinction between the formulas
\neg\neg A
and
A
’).
This is a property that cannot be shown, but has to forced onto the system, and can cause havoc for proof contraction.

There are many different rules that express this to a different degree, like:

\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{\dquad}c@{\dquad}c@{\dquad}c@{\dquad}c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\penalty{\Gamma\kern-0.975pt,\neg A}\mathrel{\vdash}{{\perp}}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=39.65137pt}
\hbox{}}}\hbox{\kern 9.89511pt\hbox{${\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{A}$}}}}\hfil\quad\quad&{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\penalty{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{\neg\neg A}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=36.04027pt}
\hbox{}}}\hbox{\kern 8.08955pt\hbox{${\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{A}$}}}}\hfil\quad\quad&{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 18.29932pt\hbox{$\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=36.11113pt}
\hbox{}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{${}\mathrel{\vdash}{A\mathbin{\vee}\neg A}$}}}}\hfil\quad\quad&{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 33.87054pt\hbox{$\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=67.25359pt}
\hbox{}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{${}\mathrel{\vdash}{((A\mathord{\rightarrow}B)\mathord{\rightarrow}A)\mathord{\rightarrow}A}$}}}}\hfil\quad\quad&{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 27.60489pt\hbox{$\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=54.72227pt}
\hbox{}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{${}\mathrel{\vdash}{(\neg A\mathord{\rightarrow}A})\mathop{\rightarrow}A$}}}}\end{array}

(called ‘
proof by contradiction
’, ‘
double negation elimination
’, ‘
law of excluded middle
’, ‘
Peirce’s law
’, and ‘
reductio ad absurdum
’, respectively.)
These rules have different expressive power, and adding one rather than another can change the set of derivable properties (see
[
AH03
]
).

1.1.
Classical Natural Deduction with Implication and Negation

The variant of Classical Natural Deduction we will consider in this paper uses the logical connectors
\neg
(
negation
) and
\mathord{\rightarrow}
(
implication
).

Definition 1.1

(Natural deduction with negation and implication)
.

The formulas we use for our system of natural deduction with negation and implication are:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}A,B&::=&{\varphi}\mid A\mathop{\rightarrow}B\mid\neg A\end{array}

where ‘
\mathord{\rightarrow}
’ associates to the right and ‘
\neg
’ binds stronger than ‘
\mathord{\rightarrow}
’, and
{\varphi}
is basic formula
3
3

3

We choose to use
{\varphi}
as a primitive formula because we aim to use these as types, using the Curry-Howard correspondence, by ‘inhabiting’ proofs with term information.
, of which there are infinitely countable many.
A context
\Gamma
is a set of formulas, where
\Gamma\kern-1.0pt,A=\Gamma\mathbin{\cup}\{\hskip 0.25pt\hbox{$A$}\hskip 0.25pt\}
and the inference rules are:

\begin{array}[]{rl@{\dquad}rl@{\dquad}rl@{\dquad}rl}({\textsl{Ax}}):&{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 16.14653pt\hbox{$\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=31.80556pt}
\hbox{}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{${\Gamma\kern-0.975pt,A}\mathrel{\vdash}{A}$}}}}\hfil\quad\quad&({\mathord{\rightarrow}\textsl{I}\hskip 1.0pt}):&{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.52156pt\hbox{$\penalty{\Gamma\kern-0.975pt,A}\mathrel{\vdash}{B}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=32.94798pt}
\hbox{}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{${\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{A\mathop{\rightarrow}B}$}}}}\hfil\quad\quad&({\mathord{\rightarrow}\textsl{E}\hskip 0.4pt}):&{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\penalty{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{A\mathop{\rightarrow}B}\quad{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{A}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=62.32162pt}
\hbox{}}}\hbox{\kern 20.93684pt\hbox{${\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{B}$}}}}\hfil\quad\quad\\[14.22636pt]
({\textsl{PbC}}):&{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\penalty{\Gamma\kern-0.975pt,\neg A}\mathrel{\vdash}{{\perp}}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=39.65137pt}
\hbox{}}}\hbox{\kern 9.89511pt\hbox{${\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{A}$}}}}\hfil\quad\quad&({\neg\textsl{I}\hskip 0.4pt}):&{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\penalty{\Gamma\kern-0.975pt,A}\mathrel{\vdash}{{\perp}}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=31.31805pt}
\hbox{}}}\hbox{\kern 1.5618pt\hbox{${\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{\neg A}$}}}}\hfil\quad\quad&({\neg\textsl{E}\hskip 0.4pt}):&{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\penalty{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{\neg A}\quad{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{A}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=57.56812pt}
\hbox{}}}\hbox{\kern 18.8535pt\hbox{${\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{{\perp}}$}}}}\hfil\quad\quad\end{array}

We write
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash_{\kern-2.0pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\scriptsize\sc ni}}}A
for judgements derivable in this system, and
\mathrel{\vdash_{\kern-2.0pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\scriptsize\sc ni}}}
as name for the system.

Notice that
{\perp}
is not a formula in
\mathrel{\vdash_{\kern-2.0pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\scriptsize\sc ni}}}
, but is a place-holder, used only to represent conflict; it could be omitted from the system, by deriving
{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{}
as the conclusion of
({\neg\textsl{E}\hskip 0.4pt})
.
Also, the weakening rule

\begin{array}[]{rl}(\textsl{Wk}):&{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 1.64653pt\hbox{$\penalty{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{A}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=22.6667pt}
\hbox{\kern 0.975pt${\mbox{$(\Gamma^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma)$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{${\Gamma^{\prime}}\mathrel{\vdash}{A}$}}}}\end{array}

is admissible.

To compare
\mathrel{\vdash_{\kern-2.0pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\scriptsize\sc ni}}}
with the logic with focus
\mathrel{\vdash_{\kern-2.0pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\scriptsize\sc f}}}
we will see below,
we show Peirce’s law in
\mathrel{\vdash_{\kern-2.0pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\scriptsize\sc ni}}}
(where
\Gamma=(A\mathop{\rightarrow}B)\mathop{\rightarrow}A,\neg A
):

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 14.32314pt\hbox{$\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=28.19444pt}
\hbox{\kern 0.90367pt${\mbox{$({\textsl{Ax}})$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\neg A$}}}}\>\penalty~\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 26.82765pt\hbox{$\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=53.22578pt}
\hbox{\kern 0.85904pt${\mbox{$({\textsl{Ax}})$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}(A\mathop{\rightarrow}B)\mathop{\rightarrow}A$}}}}\>\penalty\kern-45.5244pt\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 30.69577pt\hbox{$\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=61.0034pt}
\hbox{\kern 0.77629pt${\mbox{$({\textsl{Ax}})$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma\kern-0.77629pt,A,\neg B\mathrel{\vdash}\neg A$}}}}\>\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 26.52426pt\hbox{$\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=52.67009pt}
\hbox{\kern 0.75688pt${\mbox{$({\textsl{Ax}})$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma\kern-0.75688pt,A,\neg B\mathrel{\vdash}A$}}}}\>\penalty$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=176.05328pt}
\hbox{\kern 0.7962pt${\mbox{$({\neg\textsl{E}\hskip 0.31847pt})$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 61.6916pt\hbox{$\Gamma\kern-0.7962pt,A,\neg B\mathrel{\vdash}{\perp}$}}}}\>\penalty$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=208.04683pt}
\hbox{\kern 0.81662pt${\mbox{$({\textsl{PbC}})$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 87.82722pt\hbox{$\Gamma\kern-0.81662pt,A\mathrel{\vdash}B$}}}}\>\penalty$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=245.82256pt}
\hbox{\kern 0.83755pt${\mbox{$({\mathord{\rightarrow}\textsl{I}\hskip 0.83755pt})$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 106.43729pt\hbox{$\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}A\mathop{\rightarrow}B$}}}}\>\penalty$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=358.05656pt}
\hbox{\kern 0.88107pt${\mbox{$({\mathord{\rightarrow}\textsl{E}\hskip 0.35242pt})$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 169.0977pt\hbox{$\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}A$}}}}\>\penalty$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=451.27559pt}
\hbox{\kern 0.92685pt${\mbox{$({\mathord{\rightarrow}\textsl{E}\hskip 0.37073pt})$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 188.26103pt\hbox{$(A\mathop{\rightarrow}B)\mathop{\rightarrow}A,\neg A\mathrel{\vdash}{\perp}$}}}}\>\penalty$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=481.58772pt}
\hbox{\kern 0.95062pt${\mbox{$({\textsl{PbC}})$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 213.55595pt\hbox{$(A\mathop{\rightarrow}B)\mathop{\rightarrow}A\mathrel{\vdash}A$}}}}\>\penalty$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=519.29472pt}
\hbox{\kern 0.975pt${\mbox{$({\mathord{\rightarrow}\textsl{I}\hskip 0.975pt})$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 226.02055pt\hbox{$\mathrel{\vdash}((A\mathop{\rightarrow}B)\mathop{\rightarrow}A)\mathop{\rightarrow}A$}}}}\end{array}

(see also Example
2.2
).

As suggested above, in the presence of
({\textsl{PbC}})
defining proof contraction is not straightforward.
Assume we have used
({\textsl{PbC}})
to show
{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{A\mathord{\rightarrow}B}
, and also have a proof for
{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{A}
; then applying
({\mathord{\rightarrow}\textsl{E}\hskip 0.4pt})
constructs a proof that appears to be contractable.
It is, however, not directly clear how to define that.

Example 1.2
.

Take the following proof in
\mathrel{\vdash_{\kern-2.0pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\scriptsize\sc ni}}}
:

({\textsl{Ax}})

{\Gamma\kern-0.83415pt,\neg(A\mathord{\rightarrow}B)}\mathrel{\vdash}{\neg(A\mathord{\rightarrow}B)}

({\neg\textsl{E}\hskip 0.37073pt})

{\Gamma\kern-0.92685pt,\neg(A\mathord{\rightarrow}B)}\mathrel{\vdash}{{\perp}}

({\textsl{PbC}})

{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{A\mathord{\rightarrow}B}

({\mathord{\rightarrow}\textsl{E}\hskip 0.38998pt})

{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{B}

It is
a priori
not clear how to contract this proof.
We would like to use the sub-derivations to be the building stones for the proof for
{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{B}
without the
({\textsl{PbC}})
-
({\mathord{\rightarrow}\textsl{E}\hskip 0.4pt})
pair, but there is no sub-derivation above the step
({\textsl{PbC}})
that has
A
as an assumption (so does not contain
{\Gamma\kern-1.0pt,A}\mathrel{\vdash}{A}
as the result of rule
({\textsl{Ax}})
), or that derives
{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{A\mathord{\rightarrow}B}
.

There are many ways around this problem presented in the literature, but at this point we just want to highlight the problem.
There are, of course, circumstances in which we can remove the
({\textsl{PbC}})
-
({\mathord{\rightarrow}\textsl{E}\hskip 0.4pt})
pair in a proof in
\mathrel{\vdash_{\kern-2.0pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\scriptsize\sc ni}}}
.

Example 1.3
.

Assume we have the following proof (where
\Gamma^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma
)
4
4

4

Notice that the context can only get smaller when applying inference rule, except when using
(\textsl{Wk})
.
:

({\textsl{Ax}})

\Gamma^{\prime}\kern-2.25917pt,\neg(A\mathord{\rightarrow}B),\neg C\mathrel{\vdash}\neg C

({\textsl{Ax}})

\Gamma\kern-0.81662pt,\neg(A\mathord{\rightarrow}B),\neg C,\neg D\mathrel{\vdash}\neg(A\mathord{\rightarrow}B)

(\textsl{Wk})

\Gamma\kern-0.7962pt,\neg(A\mathord{\rightarrow}B),\neg C,\neg D\mathrel{\vdash}A\mathord{\rightarrow}B

({\neg\textsl{E}\hskip 0.335pt})

\Gamma\kern-0.83755pt,\neg(A\mathord{\rightarrow}B),\neg C,\neg D\mathrel{\vdash}{\perp}

({\textsl{PbC}})

\Gamma\kern-0.85904pt,\neg(A\mathord{\rightarrow}B),\neg C\mathrel{\vdash}D

({\neg\textsl{E}\hskip 0.37073pt})

\Gamma^{\prime}\kern-2.31712pt,\neg(A\mathord{\rightarrow}B),\neg C\mathrel{\vdash}{\perp}

({\textsl{PbC}})

\Gamma^{\prime}\kern-2.37656pt,\neg C\mathrel{\vdash}A\mathord{\rightarrow}B

({\mathord{\rightarrow}\textsl{E}\hskip 0.38998pt})

\Gamma^{\prime}\kern-2.43752pt,\neg C\mathrel{\vdash}B

then we can bring sub-proof
D_{3}
to the right of sub-proof
D_{1}
, apply
({\mathord{\rightarrow}\textsl{E}\hskip 0.4pt})
, and construct the proof

({\textsl{Ax}})

\Gamma^{\prime}\kern-2.31712pt,\neg B,\neg C\mathrel{\vdash}\neg C

({\textsl{Ax}})

\Gamma\kern-0.83755pt,\neg B,\neg C,\neg D\mathrel{\vdash}\neg B

({\mathord{\rightarrow}\textsl{E}\hskip 0.31847pt})

\Gamma^{\prime}\kern-1.99051pt,\neg C\mathrel{\vdash}B

(\textsl{Wk})

\Gamma\kern-0.81662pt,\neg B,\neg C,\neg D\mathrel{\vdash}B

({\neg\textsl{E}\hskip 0.3436pt})

\Gamma\kern-0.85904pt,\neg B,\neg C,\neg D\mathrel{\vdash}{\perp}

({\textsl{PbC}})

\Gamma\kern-0.88107pt,\neg B,\neg C\mathrel{\vdash}D

({\neg\textsl{E}\hskip 0.38023pt})

\Gamma^{\prime}\kern-2.37656pt,\neg B,\neg C\mathrel{\vdash}{\perp}

({\textsl{PbC}})

\Gamma^{\prime}\kern-2.43752pt,\neg C\mathrel{\vdash}B

whereby removing the
({\textsl{PbC}})
-
({\mathord{\rightarrow}\textsl{E}\hskip 0.4pt})
pair; the derivation
D_{2}^{\prime}
is in structure equal to
D_{2}
, in the sense that the same rules get applied in the same order.
Since we have removed the
\mathop{\rightarrow}
-type, we can argue that the complexity of the proof has decreased.
We will see below (Example
2.3
and
2.10
) that this kind of proof contraction gets successfully modelled in
{\lambda\mu}
.

We will see in Section
2.3
a term calculus that directly represents proofs in
\mathrel{\vdash_{\kern-2.0pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\scriptsize\sc ni}}}
, and presents a notion of reduction that represents the above proof contraction, by presenting a different kind of term substitution.

To better be able to reason about the structure of proofs and the technicalities of proof contraction, we need to represent the structure of proofs via term information from an appropriate calculus, and inhabit the inference rules with terms, such that proof contractions will come to correspond to term reduction.
This employs the Curry-Howard principle, which expresses a correspondence between terms and their types on one side, and proofs for propositions on the other.
We will see below that associating a term calculus to an inference system unlocks the subtle differences between the variants of Classical Logic we consider here.

The natural way to inhabit
\mathrel{\vdash_{\kern-2.0pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\scriptsize\sc ni}}}
is using Summer’s
\nu\lambda\mu

[
Sum08
]
; we will first present Parigot’s calculus
{\lambda\mu}

[
Par93b
]
, as this historically came first, and gives a very elegant solution to the proof-contraction problem of Example
1.2
.

2.
The foundation of
{\lambda\mu}

Parigot’s
{\lambda\mu}
-calculus is a proof-term syntax for classical logic, expressed in Natural Deduction, defined as an extension of the Curry type assignment system for
the
\lambda
-calculus
.
With
{\lambda\mu}
Parigot created a multi-conclusion typing system which corresponds to a classical logic with
focus
; there derivable statements have the shape
{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{A}\mathrel{\mid}{\Delta}\hskip 0.5pt
, where
A
is the main conclusion of the statement, expressed as the
active
conclusion,
\Gamma
is the set of assumptions and
\Delta
is the set of alternative conclusions, or have the shape
{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{{\perp}}\mathrel{\mid}{\Delta}\hskip 0.5pt
if there is no formula under focus.

2.1.
A classical logic with focus

Before discussing
{\lambda\mu}
, in order to better compare it with the other calculi we discuss in this paper, we first revise its underlying logic, which corresponds to the following system.

Definition 2.1

(A classical logic with focus)
.

The formulas for this system are:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}A,B&::=&{\varphi}\mid A\mathop{\rightarrow}B\end{array}

Contexts
\Gamma
and co-contexts
\Delta
are sets of formulas, and the inference rules are defined through:

\begin{array}[]{rl@{\dquad}rl@{\dquad}rl}({\textsl{Ax}}):&{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 21.66808pt\hbox{$\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=42.84865pt}
\hbox{}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{${\Gamma\kern-0.975pt,A}\mathrel{\vdash}{A}\mathrel{\mid}{\Delta}\hskip 0.4875pt$}}}}\hfil\quad\quad&({\textsl{Act}\hskip 0.4pt}):&{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\penalty{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{{\perp}}\mathrel{\mid}{A,\Delta}\hskip 0.4875pt$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=42.36115pt}
\hbox{}}}\hbox{\kern 5.72845pt\hbox{${\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{A}\mathrel{\mid}{\Delta}\hskip 0.4875pt$}}}}\hfil\quad\quad&({\textsl{Pass}\hskip 0.4pt}):&{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.24374pt\hbox{$\penalty{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{A}\mathrel{\mid}{A,\Delta}\hskip 0.4875pt$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=42.84865pt}
\hbox{}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{${\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{{\perp}}\mathrel{\mid}{A,\Delta}\hskip 0.4875pt$}}}}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{rl@{\dquad}rl}({\mathord{\rightarrow}\textsl{I}\hskip 1.0pt}):&{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.52156pt\hbox{$\penalty{\Gamma\kern-0.975pt,A}\mathrel{\vdash}{B}\mathrel{\mid}{\Delta}\hskip 0.4875pt$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=43.99107pt}
\hbox{}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{${\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{A\mathop{\rightarrow}B}\mathrel{\mid}{\Delta}\hskip 0.4875pt$}}}}\hfil\quad\quad&({\mathord{\rightarrow}\textsl{E}\hskip 0.4pt}):&{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\penalty{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{A\mathop{\rightarrow}B}\mathrel{\mid}{\Delta}\hskip 0.4875pt\quad{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{A}\mathrel{\mid}{\Delta}\hskip 0.4875pt$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=84.4078pt}
\hbox{}}}\hbox{\kern 26.45839pt\hbox{${\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{B}\mathrel{\mid}{\Delta}\hskip 0.4875pt$}}}}\end{array}

We write
{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash_{\kern-2.0pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\scriptsize\sc f}}}{M:A}\mathrel{\mid}{\Delta}\hskip 0.5pt
for judgements derivable in this system.

Notice that, as above,
{\perp}
is not a formula in
\mathrel{\vdash_{\kern-2.0pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\scriptsize\sc f}}}
, but only represents conflict; also, the formulas in
\Delta
are interpreted as
negated
.
The rule
({\textsl{Act}\hskip 0.4pt})
(
activation
) moves the focus onto the formula
A
, whereas the rule
({\textsl{Pass}\hskip 0.4pt})
(
passivation
) removes the focus of a proof, since it is in contradiction with the co-context.
It will be clear that, given that
{\perp}
is not a formula, the rule
({\textsl{Pass}\hskip 0.4pt})
can only be followed by the rule
({\textsl{Act}\hskip 0.4pt})
, which together change the focus of the proof.

Example 2.2
.

Notice that negation is not part of the type language, so does not occur in
\Gamma
nor in
\Delta
.
It is therefore not possible to show
({\hbox{{{dne}}}})
in this logic (not without first extending the syntax of formulas, see Example
2.11
); however, it is possible to show Peirce’s law:

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 50.71147pt\hbox{$\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=100.9824pt}
\hbox{\kern 0.88107pt${\mbox{$({\textsl{Ax}})$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{${(A\mathop{\rightarrow}B)\mathop{\rightarrow}A}\mathrel{\vdash_{\kern-1.76215pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\sc f}}}{(A\mathop{\rightarrow}B)\mathop{\rightarrow}A}\mathrel{\mid}{A}\hskip 0.44054pt$}}}}\>\penalty\quad\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 46.22453pt\hbox{$\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=92.05096pt}
\hbox{\kern 0.7962pt${\mbox{$({\textsl{Ax}})$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{${(A\mathop{\rightarrow}B)\mathop{\rightarrow}A,A}\mathrel{\vdash_{\kern-1.5924pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\sc f}}}{A}\mathrel{\mid}{A,B}\hskip 0.3981pt$}}}}\>\penalty$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=123.0316pt}
\hbox{\kern 0.81662pt${\mbox{$({\textsl{Pass}\hskip 0.32663pt})$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 15.48521pt\hbox{${(A\mathop{\rightarrow}B)\mathop{\rightarrow}A,A}\mathrel{\vdash_{\kern-1.63324pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\sc f}}}{{\perp}}\mathrel{\mid}{A,B}\hskip 0.40831pt$}}}}\>\penalty$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=160.96727pt}
\hbox{\kern 0.83755pt${\mbox{$({\textsl{Act}\hskip 0.335pt})$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 40.42001pt\hbox{${(A\mathop{\rightarrow}B)\mathop{\rightarrow}A,A}\mathrel{\vdash_{\kern-1.67511pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\sc f}}}{B}\mathrel{\mid}{A}\hskip 0.41878pt$}}}}\>\penalty$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=195.31725pt}
\hbox{\kern 0.85904pt${\mbox{$({\mathord{\rightarrow}\textsl{I}\hskip 0.85904pt})$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 57.31183pt\hbox{${(A\mathop{\rightarrow}B)\mathop{\rightarrow}A}\mathrel{\vdash_{\kern-1.71808pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\sc f}}}{A\mathop{\rightarrow}B}\mathrel{\mid}{A}\hskip 0.42952pt$}}}}\>\penalty$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=365.31808pt}
\hbox{\kern 0.90367pt${\mbox{$({\mathord{\rightarrow}\textsl{E}\hskip 0.36145pt})$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 148.84451pt\hbox{${(A\mathop{\rightarrow}B)\mathop{\rightarrow}A}\mathrel{\vdash_{\kern-1.80734pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\sc f}}}{A}\mathrel{\mid}{A}\hskip 0.45183pt$}}}}\>\penalty$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=395.63283pt}
\hbox{\kern 0.92685pt${\mbox{$({\textsl{Pass}\hskip 0.37073pt})$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 163.9961pt\hbox{${(A\mathop{\rightarrow}B)\mathop{\rightarrow}A}\mathrel{\vdash_{\kern-1.8537pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\sc f}}}{{\perp}}\mathrel{\mid}{A}\hskip 0.46342pt$}}}}\>\penalty$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=433.55606pt}
\hbox{\kern 0.95062pt${\mbox{$({\textsl{Act}\hskip 0.38023pt})$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 186.70177pt\hbox{${(A\mathop{\rightarrow}B)\mathop{\rightarrow}A}\mathrel{\vdash_{\kern-1.90125pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\sc f}}}{A}\mathrel{\mid}{}\hskip 0.47531pt$}}}}\>\penalty$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=467.89328pt}
\hbox{\kern 0.975pt${\mbox{$({\mathord{\rightarrow}\textsl{I}\hskip 0.975pt})$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 197.47537pt\hbox{${}\mathrel{\vdash_{\kern-1.95001pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\sc f}}}{((A\mathop{\rightarrow}B)\mathop{\rightarrow}A)\mathop{\rightarrow}A}\mathrel{\mid}{}\hskip 0.4875pt$}}}}\end{array}

The intention of this system is to express classical logic, and for this it encapsulates the rule
({\textsl{PbC}})
.
Since the formulas in
\Delta
are seen as negated, any statement
{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash_{\kern-2.0pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\scriptsize\sc f}}}{A}\mathrel{\mid}{\Delta}\hskip 0.5pt
can be seen as
\Gamma\kern-1.0pt,\neg\Delta\mathrel{\vdash_{\kern-2.0pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\scriptsize\sc ni}}}A
(where
\neg\Delta
lists the negated versions of all types in
\Delta
).
With that view, the rules
({\textsl{Act}\hskip 0.4pt})
and
({\textsl{Pass}\hskip 0.4pt})
corresponds to allowing the following variants of rules
({\textsl{PbC}})
and
({\neg\textsl{E}\hskip 0.4pt})

\begin{array}[]{c@{\qquad}c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\penalty{\Gamma\kern-0.975pt,\neg\Delta,\neg A}\mathrel{\vdash}{{\perp}}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=60.76245pt}
\hbox{\kern 0.975pt${\mbox{$({\textsl{PbC}})$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 9.89511pt\hbox{${\Gamma\kern-0.975pt,\neg\Delta}\mathrel{\vdash}{A}$}}}}\hfil\quad\quad\quad\quad&{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 35.0293pt\hbox{$\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=69.58328pt}
\hbox{\kern 0.95062pt${\mbox{$({\textsl{Ax}})$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{${\Gamma\kern-0.95062pt,\neg\Delta,\neg A}\mathrel{\vdash}{\neg A}$}}}}\>\penalty\quad{\Gamma\kern-0.95062pt,\neg\Delta,\neg A}\mathrel{\vdash}{A}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=171.73471pt}
\hbox{\kern 0.975pt${\mbox{$({\neg\textsl{E}\hskip 0.38998pt})$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 55.24239pt\hbox{${\Gamma\kern-0.975pt,\neg\Delta,\neg A}\mathrel{\vdash}{{\perp}}$}}}}\end{array}

but in a version of Natural Deduction where formulas have at most a negation at the front.
Note that it therefore avoides the problem of Example
1.2
by not allowing the rule
({\textsl{PbC}})
to be applied to assumptions on the right in
({\neg\textsl{E}\hskip 0.4pt})
:
A
cannot be a negated type, so the premises in the right-hand proof cannot occur swapped.

Example 2.3
.

Using the above observation, following from Example
1.3
, we can create the proofs in
\mathrel{\vdash_{\kern-2.0pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\scriptsize\sc f}}}
, and contract the left proof into the right one.

(\textsl{Wk})

{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{A\mathord{\rightarrow}B}\mathrel{\mid}{A\mathord{\rightarrow}B,C,D,\Delta}\hskip 0.41878pt

({\textsl{Pass}\hskip 0.3436pt})

{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{{\perp}}\mathrel{\mid}{A\mathord{\rightarrow}B,C,D,\Delta}\hskip 0.42952pt

({\textsl{Act}\hskip 0.35242pt})

{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{D}\mathrel{\mid}{A\mathord{\rightarrow}B,C,\Delta}\hskip 0.44054pt

({\textsl{Pass}\hskip 0.37073pt})

{\Gamma^{\prime}}\mathrel{\vdash}{{\perp}}\mathrel{\mid}{A\mathord{\rightarrow}B,C,\Delta}\hskip 0.46342pt

({\textsl{Act}\hskip 0.38023pt})

{\Gamma^{\prime}}\mathrel{\vdash}{A\mathord{\rightarrow}B}\mathrel{\mid}{C,\Delta}\hskip 0.47531pt

({\mathord{\rightarrow}\textsl{E}\hskip 0.38998pt})

{\Gamma^{\prime}}\mathrel{\vdash}{B}\mathrel{\mid}{C,\Delta}\hskip 0.4875pt

({\mathord{\rightarrow}\textsl{E}\hskip 0.32663pt})

{\Gamma^{\prime}}\mathrel{\vdash}{B}\mathrel{\mid}{C,\Delta}\hskip 0.40831pt

(\textsl{Wk})

{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{B}\mathrel{\mid}{B,C,D,\Delta}\hskip 0.41878pt

({\textsl{Pass}\hskip 0.3436pt})

{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{{\perp}}\mathrel{\mid}{B,C,D,\Delta}\hskip 0.42952pt

({\textsl{Act}\hskip 0.35242pt})

{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{D}\mathrel{\mid}{B,C,\Delta}\hskip 0.44054pt

({\textsl{Pass}\hskip 0.37073pt})

{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{{\perp}}\mathrel{\mid}{B,C,\Delta}\hskip 0.46342pt

({\textsl{Act}\hskip 0.38023pt})

{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{B}\mathrel{\mid}{C,\Delta}\hskip 0.47531pt

We will see in Example
2.10
that this forms the basis of structural reduction in
{\lambda\mu}
.

2.2.
The
{\lambda\mu}
-calculus

We now present the variant of
{\lambda\mu}
we consider in this paper, as defined by Parigot in
[
Par93a
]
and that gives a Curry-Howard interpretation to the inference rules of
\mathrel{\vdash_{\kern-2.0pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\scriptsize\sc f}}}
.

Definition 2.4

(Syntax of
{\lambda\mu}
)
.

Let
x
range over the infinite, countable set of
term-variables
, and
\alpha,\beta
range over the infinite, countable set of
names
.
The
{\lambda\mu}
-
terms
we consider are defined by the grammar:

\begin{array}[]{rcl@{\quad}l}M,N&::=&V\mid{M}\hskip 0.5ptN\mid\mu\hskip 0.25pt\alpha\hskip 0.25pt{.}\hskip 0.25pt[\beta]\hskip 0.5ptM\\
V&::=&x\mid\lambda x\hskip 0.25pt{.}\hskip 0.25ptM&(\emph{values})\end{array}

Recognising both
\lambda
and
\mu
as binders, the notion of free and bound names and variables of
M
,
\textit{fv}\hskip 0.25pt({M})
and
\textit{fn}({M})
, respectively, is defined as usual, and we accept Barendregt’s convention to keep free and bound names and variables distinct, using (silent)
\alpha
-conversion whenever necessary.

We write
x\mathbin{\in}M
(
\alpha\mathbin{\in}M
) if
x
(
\alpha
) occurs in
M
, either free of bound, and call a term
closed
if it has no free names or variables.
We will call the pseudo-terms of the shape
[\alpha]M

commands
, and write
C
, and treat them as terms for reasons of brevity, whenever convenient.

We will use these notations for other calculi as well in this paper.

As with Implicative Intuitionistic Logic, the reduction rules for the terms that represent the proofs correspond to proof contractions, but in
\mathrel{\vdash_{\kern-2.0pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\scriptsize\sc f}}}
.
The reduction rules for
the
\lambda
-calculus
are the
logical
reductions,
i.e.
deal with the removal of a introduction-elimination pair for implication and in addition to these, Parigot expresses also the
structural
rules that change the focus of a proof, where elimination essentially deals with negation and takes place for a type constructor that appears in one of the alternative conclusions (the Greek variable is the name given to a subterm).
Parigot therefore needs to express that the focus of the derivation (proof) changes (see the rules in Definition
2.7
), and this is achieved by extending the syntax with two new constructs
[\alpha]M
and
\mu\hskip 0.5pt{\alpha}\hskip 0.25pt{.}\hskip 0.25ptM
5
5

5

Notice that these constructs are
pseudo
terms and that they always occur together in terms.
that act as witness to
passivation
and
activation
of
\mathrel{\vdash_{\kern-2.0pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\scriptsize\sc f}}}
, which together move the focus of the derivation, and together are called a
context switch
.

Parigot defines a notion of reduction on these terms, expressed via implicit substitution, and as usual,
M\hskip 0.75pt\{\hskip-0.5ptN{/}x\hskip-0.5pt\}
stands for the (instantaneous) substitution of all occurrences of
x
in
M
by
N
.
Two kinds of structural substitution are defined: the first is the standard one, where
M\hskip 0.75pt\{\hskip-0.5pt{N}{\mathop{\cdot}}{\gamma}{/}\alpha\hskip-0.5pt\}
stands for the term obtained from
M
in which every command of the form
[\alpha]P
is replaced by
[\gamma]PN
(here
\gamma
is a fresh name).
This yields a reduction that is Call by Name (
cbn
) in nature, and shown by Py
[
Py98
]
to be confluent.

The second will be of use for Call-by-Value (
cbv
) reduction, where
\{\hskip-0.5ptN{\mathop{\cdot}}\gamma{/}\alpha\hskip-0.5pt\}\hskip 0.75ptM
stands for the term obtained from
M
in which every
[\alpha]P
is replaced by
[\gamma]NP
.
Although
cbv
is not considered for the calculus
{\mathcal{L}}
we define in Section
3
, we add its definition here, since it does form part of Summer’s calculus that we discuss in Section
2.3
, and forms an intermediate stage between
{\lambda\mu}
and
{\mathcal{L}}
.

They are formally defined by:

Definition 2.5

(Structural substitution)
.

Right-structural substitution
,
M\hskip 0.75pt\{\hskip-0.5pt{N}{\mathop{\cdot}}{\gamma}{/}\alpha\hskip-0.5pt\}
, and
left-structural substitution
,
\{\hskip-0.5ptN{\mathop{\cdot}}\gamma{/}\alpha\hskip-0.5pt\}\hskip 0.75ptM
, are defined inductively over (pseudo) terms.
The important cases are:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}[\alpha]M\hskip 0.75pt\{\hskip-0.5pt{N}{\mathop{\cdot}}{\gamma}{/}\alpha\hskip-0.5pt\}&\mathrel{\hskip 1.0pt\hbox{\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$=$}\kern-3.8889pt\kern-3.28473pt\raise 4.25pt\hbox{\hbox{{\scriptsize$\Delta$}}}\kern-3.28473pt\kern 3.8889pt}\hskip 1.0pt}&[\gamma](M\hskip 0.75pt\{\hskip-0.5pt{N}{\mathop{\cdot}}{\gamma}{/}\alpha\hskip-0.5pt\}N)\\
{}[\beta]M\hskip 0.75pt\{\hskip-0.5pt{N}{\mathop{\cdot}}{\gamma}{/}\alpha\hskip-0.5pt\}&\mathrel{\hskip 1.0pt\hbox{\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$=$}\kern-3.8889pt\kern-3.28473pt\raise 4.25pt\hbox{\hbox{{\scriptsize$\Delta$}}}\kern-3.28473pt\kern 3.8889pt}\hskip 1.0pt}&[\beta](M\hskip 0.75pt\{\hskip-0.5pt{N}{\mathop{\cdot}}{\gamma}{/}\alpha\hskip-0.5pt\})~(\beta\not=\alpha)\end{array}\quad\begin{array}[]{lcl}\{\hskip-0.5ptN{\mathop{\cdot}}\gamma{/}\alpha\hskip-0.5pt\}\hskip 0.75pt[\alpha]M&\mathrel{\hskip 1.0pt\hbox{\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$=$}\kern-3.8889pt\kern-3.28473pt\raise 4.25pt\hbox{\hbox{{\scriptsize$\Delta$}}}\kern-3.28473pt\kern 3.8889pt}\hskip 1.0pt}&[\gamma]N(\{\hskip-0.5ptN{\mathop{\cdot}}\gamma{/}\alpha\hskip-0.5pt\}\hskip 0.75ptM)\\
\{\hskip-0.5ptN{\mathop{\cdot}}\gamma{/}\alpha\hskip-0.5pt\}\hskip 0.75pt[\beta]M&\mathrel{\hskip 1.0pt\hbox{\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$=$}\kern-3.8889pt\kern-3.28473pt\raise 4.25pt\hbox{\hbox{{\scriptsize$\Delta$}}}\kern-3.28473pt\kern 3.8889pt}\hskip 1.0pt}&[\beta]\{\hskip-0.5ptN{\mathop{\cdot}}\gamma{/}\alpha\hskip-0.5pt\}\hskip 0.75ptM~(\beta\not=\alpha)\end{array}

[
Par92
]
only defines the first variant of these notions of structural substitutions (so does not use the prefix ‘right’); the two notions are defined together, but rather informally, using a notion of contexts in
[
OS97
]
.

We have the following notions of reduction on
{\lambda\mu}
.
For the fourth, call by value, different variants exists in the literature; we adopt the one from
[
OS97
]
.

Definition 2.6

(
{\lambda\mu}
reduction)
.

(1)

The reduction rules of
{\lambda\mu}
are:

\begin{array}[]{rrcl@{\hspace{6mm}}l}\textit{logical }(\beta):&({\lambda x\hskip 0.25pt{.}\hskip 0.25ptM})\hskip 0.5ptN&\mathrel{{\rightarrow}}&M\hskip 0.75pt\{\hskip-0.5ptN{/}x\hskip-0.5pt\}\hfil\hskip 17.07164pt\\
\textit{structural }(\mu):&({\mu\hskip 0.5pt{\alpha}\hskip 0.25pt{.}\hskip 0.25pt\textsl{\rm C}})\hskip 0.5ptN&\mathrel{{\rightarrow}}&\mu\hskip 0.5pt{\gamma}\hskip 0.25pt{.}\hskip 0.25pt\textsl{\rm C}\hskip 0.75pt\{\hskip-0.5pt{N}{\mathop{\cdot}}{\gamma}{/}\alpha\hskip-0.5pt\}\hfil\hskip 17.07164pt&(\gamma\textit{ fresh})\\
\textit{erasing }(\theta):&\mu\hskip 0.25pt\alpha\hskip 0.25pt{.}\hskip 0.25pt[\alpha]\hskip 0.5ptM&\mathrel{{\rightarrow}}&M\hfil\hskip 17.07164pt&(\alpha\mathbin{\not\in}M)\\
\textit{renaming }(\rho):&\mu\hskip 0.25pt\alpha\hskip 0.25pt{.}\hskip 0.25pt[\beta]\hskip 0.5pt{\mu\hskip 0.25pt\gamma\hskip 0.25pt{.}\hskip 0.25pt[\delta]\hskip 0.5ptM}&\mathrel{{\rightarrow}}&\lx@intercol\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{\,}lllll}\mu\hskip 0.25pt\alpha\hskip 0.25pt{.}\hskip 0.25pt[\beta]\hskip 0.5ptM\hskip 0.75pt\{\hskip-0.5pt\beta{/}\gamma\hskip-0.5pt\}&(\delta=\gamma)\\
\mu\hskip 0.25pt\alpha\hskip 0.25pt{.}\hskip 0.25pt[\delta]\hskip 0.5ptM\hskip 0.75pt\{\hskip-0.5pt\beta{/}\gamma\hskip-0.5pt\}&(\delta\not=\gamma)\end{array}\right.\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}

(2)

Evaluation contexts are defined as terms with a single hole
{\lceil}\hskip 1.0pt\hskip 1.0pt{\rfloor}
by:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\hbox{{{C}}}&::=&{\lceil}\hskip 1.0pt\hskip 1.0pt{\rfloor}\mid{\hbox{{{C}}}}\hskip 0.5ptM\mid{M}\hskip 0.5pt{\hbox{{{C}}}}\mid\lambda x\hskip 0.25pt{.}\hskip 0.25pt{\hbox{{{C}}}}\mid\mu\hskip 0.25pt\alpha\hskip 0.25pt{.}\hskip 0.25pt[\beta]\hskip 0.5pt\hbox{{{C}}}\end{array}

We write
\hbox{{{C}}}\hskip 0.75pt{{\lceil}\hskip 1.0pt{M}\hskip 1.0pt{\rfloor}}
for the term obtained by replacing the hole with the term
M
.

(Free, unconstrained) reduction
\mathrel{{\rightarrow}\kern-0.25pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{\scriptsize$\beta\kern-0.4pt\mu$}}
on
{\lambda\mu}
-terms is defined through

\hbox{{{C}}}\hskip 0.75pt{{\lceil}\hskip 1.0pt{M}\hskip 1.0pt{\rfloor}}\mathrel{\mathord{\rightarrow}_{\kern-0.5pt\mbox{{\sc n}}}}\hbox{{{C}}}\hskip 0.75pt{{\lceil}\hskip 1.0pt{N}\hskip 1.0pt{\rfloor}}
if
M\mathrel{{\rightarrow}}N
using either the
\beta
,
\mu
,
\theta
, or
\rho
-reductions rule.

(3)

Call by Name (
cbn
) evaluation contexts
are defined as:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}{\hbox{{{C}}}_{\mbox{{\sc n}}}}&::=&{\lceil}\hskip 1.0pt\hskip 1.0pt{\rfloor}\mid{{\hbox{{{C}}}_{\mbox{{\sc n}}}}}\hskip 0.5ptM\mid\mu\hskip 0.25pt\alpha\hskip 0.25pt{.}\hskip 0.25pt[\beta]\hskip 0.5pt{\hbox{{{C}}}_{\mbox{{\sc n}}}}\end{array}

cbn
reduction
\mathrel{\mathord{\rightarrow}_{\kern-0.5pt\mbox{{\sc n}}}}
is defined through:

{\hbox{{{C}}}_{\mbox{{\sc n}}}\hskip 0.75pt{{\lceil}\hskip 1.0pt{M}\hskip 1.0pt{\rfloor}}}\mathrel{\mathord{\rightarrow}_{\kern-0.5pt\mbox{{\sc n}}}}{\hbox{{{C}}}_{\mbox{{\sc n}}}\hskip 0.75pt{{\lceil}\hskip 1.0pt{N}\hskip 1.0pt{\rfloor}}}
if
M\mathrel{{\rightarrow}}N
using either the
\beta
,
\mu
,
\theta
, or
\rho
-reduction rule.

(4)

Call by Value (
cbv
) evaluation contexts
are defined through:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}{\hbox{{{C}}}_{\mbox{{\sc v}}}}&::=&{\lceil}\hskip 1.0pt\hskip 1.0pt{\rfloor}\mid{{\hbox{{{C}}}_{\mbox{{\sc v}}}}}\hskip 0.5ptM\mid{V}\hskip 0.5pt{{\hbox{{{C}}}_{\mbox{{\sc v}}}}}\mid\mu\hskip 0.25pt\alpha\hskip 0.25pt{.}\hskip 0.25pt[\beta]\hskip 0.5pt{\hbox{{{C}}}_{\mbox{{\sc v}}}}\end{array}

cbv
reduction
\mathrel{\mathord{\rightarrow}_{\kern-0.5pt\mbox{{\sc v}}}}
is defined through:

{\hbox{{{C}}}_{\mbox{{\sc v}}}\hskip 0.75pt{{\lceil}\hskip 1.0pt{M}\hskip 1.0pt{\rfloor}}}\mathrel{\mathord{\rightarrow}_{\kern-0.5pt\mbox{{\sc v}}}}{\hbox{{{C}}}_{\mbox{{\sc v}}}\hskip 0.75pt{{\lceil}\hskip 1.0pt{N}\hskip 1.0pt{\rfloor}}}
if
M\mathrel{{\rightarrow}}N
using either
\mu
,
\theta
,
\rho
, or:

\begin{array}[t]{rrcl@{\quad}l}(\beta_{\mbox{{\sc v}}}):&({\lambda x\hskip 0.25pt{.}\hskip 0.25ptM})\hskip 0.5ptV&\mathrel{\mathord{\rightarrow}_{\kern-0.5pt\mbox{{\sc v}}}}&M\hskip 0.75pt\{\hskip-0.5ptV{/}x\hskip-0.5pt\}\\
(\mu_{\mbox{{\sc v}}}):&{V}\hskip 0.5pt({\mu\hskip 0.5pt{\alpha}\hskip 0.25pt{.}\hskip 0.25pt\textsl{\rm C}})&\mathrel{\mathord{\rightarrow}_{\kern-0.5pt\mbox{{\sc v}}}}&\mu\hskip 0.5pt{\gamma}\hskip 0.25pt{.}\hskip 0.25pt\{\hskip-0.5ptV{\mathop{\cdot}}\gamma{/}\alpha\hskip-0.5pt\}\hskip 0.75pt\textsl{\rm C}&(\gamma\textit{ fresh})\end{array}

Remark that, for rule
(\mu_{\mbox{{\sc v}}})
,
\mu\hskip 0.25pt\alpha\hskip 0.25pt{.}\hskip 0.25pt[\beta]\hskip 0.5ptN
is not a value.
Also, unlike for the
\lambda
-calculus,
cbv
reduction is not a sub-reduction system of
\mathrel{{\rightarrow}\kern-0.25pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{\scriptsize$\beta\kern-0.4pt\mu$}}
: the rule
(\mu_{\mbox{{\sc v}}})
(and left-structural substitution) are not part of
\mathrel{{\rightarrow}\kern-0.25pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{\scriptsize$\beta\kern-0.4pt\mu$}}
.
Both
cbn
and
cbv
constitute
reduction strategies
in that they pick exactly one
\beta\mu
-redex to contract; notice that a term might be in either
cbn
or
cbv
-normal form (
i.e.
reduction has stopped), but need not be that for
\mathrel{{\rightarrow}\kern-0.25pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{\scriptsize$\beta\kern-0.4pt\mu$}}
.

Type assignment for
{\lambda\mu}
is defined below through inhabiting the inference rules of
\mathrel{\vdash_{\kern-2.0pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\scriptsize\sc f}}}
with syntax; there is a
main
, or
active
, conclusion, labelled by a term, and the
alternative
conclusions are labelled by names
\alpha
,
\beta
,
etc
.
Judgements in
{\lambda\mu}
are of the shape
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash_{\kern-1.0pt\lambda\kern-1.0pt\mu}}{M}\colon{A}\mathrel{\mid}{\Delta}\hskip 0.5pt
, where
\Delta
consists of pairs of Greek characters (the
names
) and types; the left-hand context
\Gamma
, as for the
\lambda
-calculus, contains pairs of Roman characters and types, and represents the types of the free term variables of
M
.

Definition 2.7

(Typing rules for
{\lambda\mu}
)
.

(1)

Let
{\varphi}
range over a countable (infinite) set of type-variables.
The set of types is defined by the grammar:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}A,B&::=&{\varphi}\mid A\mathop{\rightarrow}B\end{array}

(2)

A
context
(of term variables)
\Gamma
is a partial mapping from term variables to types, denoted as a finite set of
statements

x\mathord{:}A
, such that the
subjects
of the statements (
x
) are distinct.
We write
\Gamma\kern-1.5pt_{1},\Gamma\kern-1.5pt_{2}
for the
compatible
union of
\Gamma\kern-1.5pt_{1}
and
\Gamma\kern-1.5pt_{2}
(if
x\mathord{:}A_{1}\mathbin{\in}\Gamma\kern-1.5pt_{1}
and
x\mathord{:}A_{2}\mathbin{\in}\Gamma\kern-1.5pt_{2}
, then
A_{1}=A_{2}
), and write
\Gamma\kern-1.0pt,x\mathord{:}A
for
\Gamma\kern-1.0pt,\{\hskip 0.25pt\hbox{$x\mathord{:}A$}\hskip 0.25pt\}
,
x\mathbin{\not\in}\Gamma
if there exists no
A
such that
x\mathord{:}A\mathbin{\in}\Gamma
, and
\Gamma\mathord{\setminus}x
for
\Gamma\mathord{\setminus}\{\hskip 0.25pt\hbox{$x\mathord{:}A$}\hskip 0.25pt\}
.

(3)

A
context of names

\Delta
(or
co-context
) is a partial mapping from
names
to types, denoted as a finite set of
statements

\alpha\mathord{:}A
, such that the
subjects
of the statements (
\alpha
) are distinct.
Notions
\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}
, as well as
\Delta,\alpha\mathord{:}A
and
\alpha\mathbin{\not\in}\Delta
,
\Delta\mathord{\setminus}\alpha
are defined as for
\Gamma
.

(4)

The type assignment rules for
{\lambda\mu}
, adapted to our notation, are:

\kern-5.69054pt\begin{array}[]{rl@{\quad}rl@{\quad}rl}({\textsl{Ax}}):&{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 30.16113pt\hbox{$\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=59.83476pt}
\hbox{}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma\kern-0.975pt,x\mathord{:}A\mathrel{\vdash}{x}\colon{A}\mathrel{\mid}{\Delta}\hskip 0.4875pt$}}}}&(\mu):&{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 3.43011pt\hbox{$\penalty\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}{M}\colon{B}\mathrel{\mid}{\alpha\mathord{:}A,\beta\mathord{:}B,\Delta}\hskip 0.4875pt$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=93.51768pt}
\hbox{\kern 0.975pt${\mbox{$(\alpha\mathbin{\not\in}\Delta)$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}{\mu\alpha.[\beta]M}\colon{A}\mathrel{\mid}{\beta\mathord{:}B,\Delta}\hskip 0.4875pt$}}}}\quad{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 4.08778pt\hbox{$\penalty\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}{M}\colon{A}\mathrel{\mid}{\alpha\mathord{:}A,\Delta}\hskip 0.47531pt$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=73.28098pt}
\hbox{\kern 0.95062pt${\mbox{$(\alpha\mathbin{\not\in}\Delta)$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}{\mu\alpha.[\alpha]M}\colon{A}\mathrel{\mid}{\Delta}\hskip 0.47531pt$}}}}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{rl@{\quad}rl}({\mathord{\rightarrow}\textsl{I}\hskip 1.0pt}):&{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 4.27155pt\hbox{$\penalty\Gamma\kern-0.975pt,x\mathord{:}A\mathrel{\vdash}{M}\colon{B}\mathrel{\mid}{\Delta}\hskip 0.4875pt$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=73.55354pt}
\hbox{\kern 0.975pt${\mbox{$(x\mathbin{\not\in}\Gamma)$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}{\lambda x.M}\colon{A\mathop{\rightarrow}B}\mathrel{\mid}{\Delta}\hskip 0.4875pt$}}}}&({\mathord{\rightarrow}\textsl{E}\hskip 0.4pt}):&{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\penalty\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}{M}\colon{A\mathop{\rightarrow}B}\mathrel{\mid}{\Delta}\hskip 0.4875pt\quad\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}{N}\colon{A}\mathrel{\mid}{\Delta}\hskip 0.4875pt$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=109.88pt}
\hbox{}}}\hbox{\kern 27.84729pt\hbox{$\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}{MN}\colon{B}\mathrel{\mid}{\Delta}\hskip 0.4875pt$}}}}\end{array}

We will write
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash_{\kern-1.0pt\lambda\kern-1.0pt\mu}}{M}\colon{A}\mathrel{\mid}{\Delta}\hskip 0.5pt
for statements derivable in this system.

(5)

We extend Barendregt’s convention on free and bound variables and names to judgements (for all the notions of type assignment we define here), so in
\Gamma\kern-1.0pt,x\mathord{:}A\mathrel{\vdash_{\kern-1.0pt\lambda\kern-1.0pt\mu}}{M}\colon{B}\mathrel{\mid}{\alpha\mathord{:}C,\Delta}\hskip 0.5pt
, both
x
and
\alpha
cannot appear bound in
M
.

We can think of
[\alpha]M
as storing the type of
M
amongst the alternative conclusions by giving it the name
\alpha
.
Notice that
{\perp}
is not used at all in
\mathrel{\vdash_{\kern-1.0pt\lambda\kern-1.0pt\mu}}
.

Notice that, if we erase all term information from the inference rules, we get the rules from
\mathrel{\vdash_{\kern-2.0pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\scriptsize\sc f}}}
, but for the variants of
(\mu)
; these we can infer, however, so they are admissible.

({\textsl{Pass}\hskip 0.38023pt})

{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{{\perp}}\mathrel{\mid}{A,B,\Delta}

({\textsl{Act}\hskip 0.38998pt})

{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{A}\mathrel{\mid}{B,\Delta}

({\textsl{Pass}\hskip 0.37073pt})

{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{{\perp}}\mathrel{\mid}{A,\Delta}

({\textsl{Act}\hskip 0.38023pt})

{\Gamma}\mathrel{\vdash}{A}\mathrel{\mid}{\Delta}

The following result is standard and of use in the proofs below.

Lemma 2.8

(Weakening and thinning for
\mathrel{\vdash_{\kern-1.0pt\lambda\kern-1.0pt\mu}}
)
.

The following rules for
weakening
and
thinning
are admissible for
\mathrel{\vdash_{\kern-1.0pt\lambda\kern-1.0pt\mu}}
:

\begin{array}[]{rl@{\dquad}rl}(\textsl{Wk}):&{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 3.0493pt\hbox{$\penalty\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}{M}\colon{A}\mathrel{\mid}{\Delta}\hskip 0.4875pt$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=50.08478pt}
\hbox{\kern 0.975pt${\mbox{$(\Gamma\subseteq\Gamma^{\prime}\kern-2.43752pt,\Delta\subseteq\Delta^{\prime})$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma^{\prime}\mathrel{\vdash}{M}\colon{A}\mathrel{\mid}{\Delta^{\prime}}\hskip 0.4875pt$}}}}\hfil\quad\quad&(\textsl{Th}):&{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 3.0493pt\hbox{$\penalty\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}{M}\colon{A}\mathrel{\mid}{\Delta}\hskip 0.4875pt$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt{\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=50.08478pt}
\hbox{\kern 0.975pt${\mbox{$(\ast)$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma^{\prime}\mathrel{\vdash}{M}\colon{A}\mathrel{\mid}{\Delta^{\prime}}\hskip 0.4875pt$}}}}\end{array}

(\ast)
:

\Gamma^{\prime}=\{\,\hbox{$x\mathord{:}B\mathbin{\in}\Gamma\mid x\mathbin{\in}\textit{fv}\hskip 0.25pt({M})$}\,\}
,
\Delta^{\prime}=\{\,\hbox{$\alpha\mathord{:}B\mathbin{\in}\Delta\mid\alpha\mathbin{\in}\textit{fn}({M})$}\,\}
.

Proof 2.9
.

Standard.∎

The following soundness result holds.

{thmC}

[
[
vB19
]
]
If
M\mathrel{{\rightarrow}\kern-0.25pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{\scriptsize$\beta\kern-0.4pt\mu$}}N
, and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash_{\kern-1.0pt\lambda\kern-1.0pt\mu}}{M}\colon{A}\mathrel{\mid}{\Delta}\hskip 0.5pt
, then
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash_{\kern-1.0pt\lambda\kern-1.0pt\mu}}{N}\colon{A}\mathrel{\mid}{\Delta}\hskip 0.5pt
.

This result is in that paper also shown for
cbv
and
cbn
-reduction.

Example 2.10
.

We can illustrate
\mu
-reduction by the derivations for the reduction step

\begin{array}[]{rcl}({\mu\hskip 0.25pt\alpha\hskip 0.25pt{.}\hskip 0.25pt[\beta]\hskip 0.5pt\hbox{{{C}}}\hskip 0.75pt{{\lceil}\hskip 1.0pt{\mu\hskip 0.25pt\delta\hskip 0.25pt{.}\hskip 0.25pt[\alpha]\hskip 0.5ptM}\hskip 1.0pt{\rfloor}}})\hskip 0.5ptN&\mathrel{{\rightarrow}\kern-0.25pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{\scriptsize$\beta\kern-0.4pt\mu$}}&\mu\hskip 0.25pt\gamma\hskip 0.25pt{.}\hskip 0.25pt[\beta]\hskip 0.5pt\hbox{{{C}}}\hskip 0.75pt{{\lceil}\hskip 1.0pt{\mu\hskip 0.25pt\delta\hskip 0.25pt{.}\hskip 0.25pt[\gamma]\hskip 0.5pt{M}\hskip 0.5ptN}\hskip 1.0pt{\rfloor}}\end{array}

(\textsl{Wk})

\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}{M}\colon{A\mathord{\rightarrow}B}\mathrel{\mid}{\alpha\mathord{:}A\mathord{\rightarrow}B,\delta\mathord{:}D,\beta\mathord{:}C,\Delta}\hskip 0.44054pt

(\mu)

\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}{\mu\hskip 0.2259pt\delta\hskip 0.2259pt{.}\hskip 0.2259pt[\alpha]\hskip 0.45183ptM}\colon{D}\mathrel{\mid}{\alpha\mathord{:}A\mathord{\rightarrow}B,\beta\mathord{:}C,\Delta}\hskip 0.45183pt

(\mu)

\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}{\mu\hskip 0.23766pt\alpha\hskip 0.23766pt{.}\hskip 0.23766pt[\beta]\hskip 0.47531pt\hskip 0.71297pt{{\lceil}\hskip 0.95062pt{\mu\hskip 0.23766pt\delta\hskip 0.23766pt{.}\hskip 0.23766pt[\alpha]\hskip 0.47531ptM}\hskip 0.95062pt{\rfloor}}}\colon{A\mathord{\rightarrow}B}\mathrel{\mid}{\beta\mathord{:}C,\Delta}\hskip 0.47531pt
